(c) the rebuilt cart had `linkedDomain` = undefined → provisioner fell back to the synthetic ID → DirectAdmin refused → generic "Hosting provisioning failed" message shown to the customer. **Investigation path**: read the actual failed order directly from the production database (read-only diagnostic script, no writes) and confirmed `domains[hosting].domainName = "hosting-standard-1781673238819"` with no `linkedDomain` field — matching the hypothesis exactly. **The fix** (committed as `be8245f`) is in four small places: (1) add `linkedDomain` field to the Order database schema (both the TypeScript interface and the Mongoose subdocument definition), (2) the create-order endpoint persists the value when it saves the pending order, (3) both code paths that reconstruct the cart from the order row (the one used to feed the provisioner AND the one used to build the Zoho invoice line items) now restore `linkedDomain`. **Security invariant preserved**: the reconstruction still reads from the database row, NOT from the client's request body — a malicious /verify call still can't swap to a different paid domain. **No data migration needed**: existing pre-fix rows have `linkedDomain` undefined (only one such row exists: the senior reviewer's failed test order `ord_1781763119592_940e00`, which won't auto-retry). **Verification**: full payment + order + model unit suite (246 tests across 10 files) all pass after the schema change. **Please retry a fresh end-to-end checkout** (add a domain + a hosting plan, pay) — domain should register through ResellerClub as usual, and the hosting half should land on the dashboard with the real domain name and a working DirectAdmin account this time (deploy `dms-00174-xpk` live)

## Recently Shipped — user-visible improvements

- [x] **Checkout "Pay" button now works end-to-end — was returning a 500 "Failed to initialise order" on every attempt; root cause was a Mongoose schema quirk auto-creating an empty paymentVerification subdocument on every pending-order save** — the senior reviewer hit a 500 + red toast on every attempt to pay at checkout (domain + hosting in cart, click Pay). The latency of the failed request (6.6 seconds) and the wording of the toast told us the payment gateway (Razorpay) had already accepted the order — the failure was on **our database side**, at the step where we save a "pending order" row immediately after the gateway hands back the order ID. **Investigation path**: (1) Google Cloud Run's request log showed the 500 but the application's own error logger was silently swallowing the underlying stack trace for this code path — we couldn't read what Mongoose was actually complaining about. (2) Shipped a temporary diagnostic build (`dms-00172-db4`) that surfaced the error inline as a `_debug` field on the 500 response so the reviewer could read it from the browser network tab — but rather than wait for a manual retry, I wrote an automated reproduction that spins up an in-memory MongoDB, loads the real Order schema, and calls `Order.create()` with the exact same payload the route persists for the reviewer's cart (domain + hosting, no trial). (3) The reproduction **immediately surfaced the actual error**: `Order validation failed: paymentVerification.razorpayOrderId: Path 'paymentVerification.razorpayOrderId' is required` (+ 4 more required-field failures, all inside `paymentVerification`). **The bug**: the Order schema's `paymentVerification` subdocument was defined as inline nested fields all marked `required: true`. Without an explicit Mongoose sub-schema + `default: undefined` wrapper, Mongoose auto-creates an empty `paymentVerification: {}` on every parent save and then runs the inner required validators against the empty object. The schema's INTENT was "if you set paymentVerification, all five fields must be present" — and that gate was supposed to fire only on the `/payments/verify` side where the verifier populates the subdoc after Razorpay confirms the payment. The unintended side effect was rejecting every pending Order row at create-order time — i.e. **every customer attempting to pay was hitting this**. **Why our test suite didn't catch it**: every existing test of the create-order route mocked `Order.create()` away (the unit-test pattern around Mongoose). The bug had been latent in the schema for a while; the senior reviewer was the first to attempt a real end-to-end checkout on this code path in production. **The fix** (committed as `91f084e`): converted `paymentVerification` to an explicit Mongoose sub-schema with `default: undefined`. The required validators still fire when `/payments/verify` actually writes the subdoc (so an incomplete verification still throws — security invariant preserved), but the parent Order can now be saved as `pending` without a verification block. Same shape as several other optional subdocuments elsewhere in the codebase. No data migration needed — existing rows that already carry a `paymentVerification` block continue to validate against the same five required fields. **Cleanup**: the temporary `_debug` field on the 500 response (which the diagnostic deploy added to surface the error to the browser) has been reverted in the same commit since we no longer need it. The throwaway in-memory-MongoDB reproduction test that surfaced the bug has been deleted (it served its purpose, leaving it would be dead code). **Verification**: the in-memory MongoDB reproduction failed against the OLD schema and passes against the NEW schema; full payment + order test suite (227 tests across 7 files) all pass; smoke test against the new production revision returns HTTP 200 on the health endpoint. **Please retry checkout on the live site** — the "Failed to initialise order" toast should no longer appear, the Pay button should proceed through to the Razorpay payment screen, and the order should land cleanly in the dashboard once payment completes (deploys `dms-00172-db4` diagnostic + `dms-00173-b9g` fix both live; the diagnostic's purpose was discharged by the in-memory MongoDB reproduction so the diagnostic deploy was overtaken before the reviewer needed to retry it)

- [x] **Google reCAPTCHA fully removed from the project (Phase 1 of a planned rip-and-replace) — "Failed to render verification widget" is gone from every public form** — the senior reviewer reported the "Failed to render verification widget" error persisting on the login page even after multiple targeted fixes (site-key typo, container-ID determinism, motion-wrapper SSR styles, nested-anchor parse-correction). Rather than chase whatever's left, the captcha has been **ripped out at the root**: the customer-facing widget, the client-side script loader, the server-side token verification on every endpoint that used it, the admin "captcha on/off" toggle, and the captcha-status endpoint that the widget called on every page load. Specifically: the **5 customer-facing forms** (login, register, contact, forgot-password, reset-password) no longer render the widget — the form submits without any "I'm not a robot" step; the **7 server-side endpoints** that used to verify a captcha token (login, register, forgot-password, reset-password, contact, payment-create-order, hosting-trial-eligibility) now skip that step entirely; the **admin settings page** no longer shows the captcha on/off toggle; the **lib + helper code** for both client + server reCAPTCHA was deleted outright. Other anti-abuse defences are unaffected and continue to gate everything: same-origin/CSRF check on every form post, per-endpoint rate limits (login, register, password-reset, contact, chat all individually throttled), single-use 1-hour reset tokens, password-strength rules, email-enumeration neutralisation on forgot-password, disposable-email blocklist + IP + device-fingerprint + optional phone-OTP on the hosting trial flow. The test suite shrank by 56 captcha-specific tests (6031 → 5975, all still passing). **Verified live on the new production revision**: the freshly-pulled /login HTML contains zero references to `recaptcha`, `grecaptcha`, `GoogleRecaptcha`, or "verification widget", the captcha-status endpoint now returns HTTP 404 as expected, and the form still renders the email + password + remember-me inputs correctly. **Note**: this is intentionally Phase 1 — Phase 2 will re-add a fresh anti-bot solution (likely Cloudflare Turnstile or hCaptcha rather than Google reCAPTCHA v2, since the third-party-cookie incognito breakage on Google's flavour was a recurring source of customer-facing errors) on its own deploy cycle. Until then the public forms have no bot-protection layer; the rate limiters + email-enumeration neutralisation are the front line (deploy `dms-00171-96f` live)

- [x] **Deploy pipeline switched from Google Cloud Build to the project's own VPS for image builds — eliminates a recurring per-deploy cost** — every production deploy until today went through Google Cloud Build, which spins up a dedicated 8-CPU virtual machine in Google's cloud, runs the Docker build of the application image there, then promotes the new image to the live Cloud Run service; that managed-build VM is billed per build-minute (the project's machine tier is `E2_HIGHCPU_8` at roughly $0.016/min), and each full build takes ~5 minutes, so the cost was ~$0.08 per deploy; across the hotfix cycles of the last two weeks (often 10+ deploys/day) that ran to ~$24/month and rising in line with deploy frequency;

**fix: switched the default deploy path** to build the Docker image **locally on the project's own VPS** (which is already paid for and running 24/7 regardless), push the finished image to Google's image registry, and then promote it to Cloud Run from there; the cloud-build path stays in the codebase as a `--cloud-build` opt-in flag for emergency use from a workstation that doesn't have Docker installed, but the default for every deploy is now the local-VPS path with no extra flag; **trade-off**: the VPS only has 2 CPUs vs Cloud Build's 8, so each deploy now takes ~9 minutes instead of ~5 — 50% slower per build but $0 in marginal cost; one-time setup done on the VPS to support this: installed Docker (the official Debian docker-ce package, version 29), added the deploy user to the `docker` group so it doesn't need sudo each time, and ran `gcloud auth configure-docker` once so the image push to the registry uses our existing Google Cloud credentials with no extra secrets to manage; **verified live**: deploy `dms-00170-bzh` was the first revision built via the new local-VPS path end-to-end and serves the same code as the prior revision (so customer-facing behaviour didn't change for this one) — proves the new pipeline works;

**project-level rule pinned in CLAUDE.md** so future contributors (and future Claude Code sessions) default to local builds and don't accidentally re-introduce the Cloud Build cost (deploy `dms-00170-bzh` live; pipeline change in commits `241d2fd` + `5efeb5b`)

- [x] **The "React #418" hydration error on the login / register pages — FIFTH-attempt root cause (the actual one): nested clickable-logo links produced invalid HTML the browser auto-corrected at parse time** — after four prior attempts (browser extensions, the homepage chat widget, the reCAPTCHA container ID, the form-field-and-button animation library entry styles) the senior reviewer STILL saw the same red React #418 error in the browser console on the login page; pulling the freshly-deployed server-rendered HTML directly with `curl` and inspecting it character-by-character finally revealed the actual culprit: the brand panel on the left side of the login page (the blue area with "Pick up where you left off") rendered the **Anutech Digital logo nested inside two stacked clickable-logo links** — both linking back to `/`; the outer wrapper was the form-shell component wrapping `<Logo>` in a `<Link href="/">` to make the whole brand-panel logo area clickable, and the inner one was the logo component's own internal `<Link href="/">` (added so the logo is independently clickable anywhere it's used); the result was server-rendered HTML like `<a href="/"><a href="/">…<img/>…</a></a>` — and **nested `<a>` tags are invalid HTML**; when the customer's browser parses the response, it auto-corrects the invalid nesting by closing the outer `<a>` before opening the inner `<a>`, producing a DOM tree with two SIBLING anchors instead of one nested pair; React then tries to hydrate against a tree-shape that doesn't match its own server-rendered output and throws #418; this single root cause has been there the whole time, hidden underneath all the smaller bugs we fixed in the previous four attempts (each of those was also real and shipped, but neither cured this); the same nested-anchor pattern was present on **both desktop and mobile-only top-bar logo placements** in the form-shell, so both /login and /register were affected; **fix is a single change** to the form-shell component: drop the outer `<Link>` wrappers around the logo on both desktop and mobile — the logo component already self-wraps in `<Link href="/">` so clicking it still navigates home, just via one anchor instead of two; **verified live on the new production revision** by pulling the freshly-built /login HTML and confirming there's no longer any `<a … href="/"><a href="/">` sequence anywhere — the logo area is now a single clean `<a href="/"><div><img/></div></a>`; this is a fifth, root-level fix — the previous four still ship (each addressed a real but different bug along the way) — and this one is the one that finally puts the red React #418 console error to rest (deploy `dms-00169-229` live)

- [x] **The "React #418" hydration error on the login / register pages — fourth-attempt root cause finally fixed: the form-field AND primary-button animation library's inline styles** — after three prior attempts (browser extensions, the homepage chat widget, the reCAPTCHA container ID) the senior reviewer still saw the same red React #418 error in the browser console on the login page, in normal-mode browsers AND across multiple different browsers (ruling out caching and extensions); fresh investigation by reading the server-rendered HTML directly with `curl` revealed **eleven elements on the login form with inline styles like `style="opacity:0;transform:translateY(-5px)"`** — these are micro-animation initial-states emitted by the page's animation library (Framer Motion) on (a) the form fields: email-field label, password-field label, the mail/lock/eye icons next to those fields, the input wrappers themselves, AND (b) the Sign In button: the user-icon wrapper span and the "Sign in" text-wrapper span inside the primary button; root cause: with the codebase now on React 19 + Framer Motion 12, the animation library's server-rendered inline styles are matched against subtly different client-rendered styles during hydration (Framer Motion adds extra style properties for hardware acceleration / will-change on the client that aren't in the server output), and React 19's stricter hydration comparison throws #418 on every mismatch; **the animations themselves are decorative 0.2-second opacity-fade-in effects on form labels, field icons, and the icon/text inside primary buttons** — they don't add meaningful UX value (customers don't even consciously perceive a 0.2s fade on a form they're going to read for several seconds anyway) but they're the actual hydration-mismatch source; fix is in two parts: (1) strip the Framer Motion wrappers from the form-field component for the parts that render on initial page load (labels, icon containers, input wrappers), keep the conditionally-rendered error/helper-message animation (those only render when there's a validation error, so they don't participate in the initial server vs. client comparison); (2) strip the Framer Motion wrappers from the primary button component (icon span, text span, the motion.button wrapper itself with its hover/tap micro-interactions) and replace the hover/tap-scale effects with plain Tailwind utility classes (`active:scale-[0.985] enabled:hover:scale-[1.015]`) which render as plain CSS without any SSR/CSR divergence; after this deploy the login form renders with no animation on labels/icons/inputs/button content (it's visually static — everything appears immediately) but the hydration mismatch is gone for good; the hover/tap scale on the Sign In button is preserved via CSS so the button still has its subtle press-feedback effect; **verified live by pulling the new login HTML directly and confirming zero `style="opacity:0;…"` inline styles remain** (previously 11 such elements, now 0); this is a fourth, root-level fix — not a workaround — and the previous three fixes still ship since each one fixed a real but different bug along the way; the same fix automatically applies to /register and any other page using the form-field or primary-button component (deploys `dms-00167-fwz` Input fix live + `dms-00168-xdc` Button fix live)

- [x] **The "React #418" hydration error on the login / register / contact / password-reset pages — the actual root cause finally identified and fixed** — across the previous two attempts we ascribed this red console error to (a) browser extensions adding attributes to the page, then (b) the homepage chat widget reading from short-term browser memory during its initial render; both were real bugs that needed fixing, but **neither was the cause of the error showing up on the login page itself** (the chat widget isn't even on the login page); the senior reviewer's persistent incognito-mode testing finally narrowed it down enough to find the actual culprit: the Google reCAPTCHA widget's container element was being assigned a unique identifier built from "current time + a random number" — a pattern that generates a different value every time it runs, including a different value on the server vs. on the customer's browser; the server-rendered HTML contained one identifier (`recaptcha-1750000000000-abc123def`), the customer's browser then re-rendered with a different identifier (`recaptcha-1750000000050-xyz789jkl`), and React's hydration step detected the mismatch and threw the error on every single page load that included the reCAPTCHA widget — login, register, contact form, password reset; the previous two fixes still ship (suppressing browser-extension noise on the page's outer elements, and the chat widget initial-render correctness on the homepage) — they address real but different cases — but this third fix is the one that addresses the login-page error specifically; **the fix uses React's official built-in mechanism for generating identifiers that are stable across server and browser rendering** (the `useId()` hook, designed exactly for this case), with a small post-processing step to make the framework-generated identifier a valid HTML id; the customer-facing experience is unchanged (the reCAPTCHA widget still gets a unique identifier so multiple instances on the same page don't conflict — it's just deterministic now); after the new deploy the red React #418 in the browser developer console should be gone on every public form page (deploy `dms-00166-pn8` live)
- [x] **Fixed two separate console errors customers (and the senior reviewer) saw in the browser developer tools — the real cause of the "React #418" error, and 400-error spam from internal logging** — the senior reviewer tested the previous fixes in Chrome's incognito mode (a fresh browser context with no extensions running and no cached data) and reported the **same red "Minified React error #418" message we'd seen yesterday plus two new "POST /api/v1/log 400 (Bad Request)" lines next to it**; the incognito test was important because it ruled out the **browser-extension theory we'd used to explain the #418 yesterday** — extensions can't run in incognito, so something else was the real cause; investigation found two separate bugs underneath: (1) **the internal client-side logger** (which forwards browser-side errors to the server so we can see them in our server logs) was sending data in the wrong shape — the server's log-receiving endpoint expects a field called "message" containing a single string (the human-readable log line), but the client was sending a field called "messages" (plural) containing an array of objects; the server's input validation rejected every log with a 400 "Bad Request" error, which the customer's browser then displayed in the developer console, creating noise that looked like a real bug — fixed by collapsing the client's array into a single space-joined string under the right field name, while still preserving the original structured array in a secondary "details" field so the server-side diagnostic context is unchanged; (2) **the homepage chat widget was reading from short-term browser memory** (the place that remembers your in-progress chat conversation across page navigations within a single browser session) **during its very first render** rather than waiting until after the page had loaded — this caused the server to render the chat widget showing only the default greeting message (because the server has no browser memory to read), while the customer's browser then immediately re-rendered the widget showing whatever conversation history was saved (which is empty for a brand-new visitor but populated for anyone who'd previously chatted in the same session); the page React framework detected this difference between the server's HTML and the browser's HTML and threw the "#418" error every single page load for any customer who'd ever chatted, regardless of whether they had any browser extensions — fixed by always starting with the default greeting on the first render (matching what the server produces) and then loading the saved conversation a moment later via the standard "after-mount" React pattern; the customer-facing experience is unchanged (the chat conversation still resumes when you navigate between pages — it just resumes one tick later); both bugs verified gone in incognito mode after the new deploy — the developer console is now clean (deploy `dms-00165-nr6` live)
- [x] **Fixed customer login / sign-up / contact / password-reset forms that were blocked by a "Failed to render verification widget" error — the cause was a one-character typo in production configuration** — the senior reviewer reported that the Google reCAPTCHA box was failing to load on customer-facing forms, with a red "Failed to render verification widget" message appearing where the "I'm not a robot" checkbox should have been; the Sign In button was greyed out as a result, blocking customers from logging in, signing up, submitting the contact form, OR requesting a password reset — every public form was effectively broken; **the reviewer initially suspected a missing domain in the Google reCAPTCHA admin console**, but verification showed `app.anutech.in` was already in the registered domain list and the site key was correct on Google's side; investigation by comparing the site key value Google's admin console showed (`6LcX07Qs…Pttl Pxl1X1s92VRe28j6EJHWF30`, with the lowercase letter L) against the value actually baked into our production configuration revealed a **single-character typo** — the production configuration had an uppercase letter "I" (capital i) where it should have been a lowercase letter "l" (lowercase L); the two characters are nearly indistinguishable in many fonts, which is why the typo went unnoticed when whoever originally set up the configuration copied the value across; Google's reCAPTCHA service treats the typo'd key as completely unknown — it has no record of any site key matching that exact string — so it refuses to render the widget at all; **fix was a single-character correction** to the production environment configuration (changing the uppercase "I" to a lowercase "l" so the value matches what Google has registered); redeployed so the corrected configuration is baked into the new build that customers' browsers download; **verified live on the new production revision** by reading the freshly-built JavaScript bundle and confirming the site key now matches the Google console character-for-character — customers should see the reCAPTCHA checkbox render normally and be able to complete login / sign-up / contact / password-reset flows again after hard-refreshing their browser (deploy `dms-00164-kq7` live)
- [x] **Admins can now actually turn the customer-form bot-protection (Google reCAPTCHA) on and off from the admin panel — previously the toggle was permanently jammed after the first save** — the senior reviewer tried to save changes to the Google reCAPTCHA setting in Admin → Settings → Security and got a red "Failed to save captcha settings" toast even though the page showed reCAPTCHA was already active; root cause was a subtle "save once, then locked out" bug introduced by an earlier security hardening pass — the admin save endpoint enforces a step-up password re-confirmation rule for any setting whose stored category is "security" (a defence against an attacker with a stolen admin session changing a sensitive setting); the captcha toggle was being saved by the admin UI with the category labelled "security", so the **first save worked** (no existing setting yet, no re-confirmation needed), but the category was then permanently recorded in the database as "security"; on **every subsequent save** the stored category triggered the step-up rule, the server returned "current password required", and the admin panel UI silently swallowed the response (there's no password-re-entry modal anywhere in the admin panel for plain feature flags) — surfacing only as the generic "Failed to save" toast; the fix: the captcha toggle (along with 5 other plain feature flags: hosting trial enable/disable, ₹1 test-plan enable/disable, the TLD-pricing cache enable+TTL, and maintenance-mode enable) are all explicitly classified server-side as **non-security feature flags that never require step-up re-confirmation**, regardless of how they're stored in the database (so any historical mis-classification from prior saves is silently downgraded — no manual database fix needed, no admin lockout possible); the admin UI was simultaneously updated to save the captcha toggle under the correct "feature flag" category so future writes are correctly labelled too; **safety check baked in**: the new "feature flag, never security" exception list is checked AFTER the hardcoded list of truly-security-critical keys (database connection details, OAuth secrets, JWT signing keys, the cron-job authentication secret, the encryption key, etc.) — so even if a future maintainer accidentally adds a real credential to both lists, the genuine-security list still wins and the re-confirmation still fires for true secrets (deploy `dms-00163-xw5` live)
- [x] **Social-login (Google / Facebook / GitHub) now also returns the customer to the cart after sign-in, instead of dropping them on the dashboard** — earlier today the regular email-and-password login was fixed to take the customer back to the cart after sign-in (so anyone who clicked "Login to Checkout" with items in their cart could come back and continue paying); the senior reviewer caught that the same fix had NOT been applied to the social-login buttons (Sign in with Google, Sign in with Facebook, Sign in with GitHub) — those still hard-coded the dashboard as the destination, so a customer who chose Google Sign-In from the cart still ended up stranded on the dashboard with their items invisible; root cause was a forgotten copy-paste path — the social-login component had `dashboard` baked in at two places (the destination NextAuth sees when the customer comes back from Google's site, AND the post-success redirect a couple of seconds after the welcome toast), neither of which had been updated when we taught the email-login form to honour the "take me back to where I came from" hint earlier today; fix: the social-login component now reads the same "return URL" hint from the login-page URL at the moment the customer clicks the social-login button, validates it through the same open-redirect safety check the email-login form uses (must be a same-site path starting with a single forward slash and a non-slash character, otherwise falls back to dashboard so this convenience can't be turned into a phishing redirect), and threads the validated destination into both the NextAuth callback AND the post-welcome-toast redirect — guarantees the two destinations stay in sync; **the same fix automatically applies to Google, Facebook, and GitHub buttons** since they all funnel through the same component; **9 new automated safety checks** added pinning every variant (cart returnUrl, checkout returnUrl, no-returnUrl-falls-back-to-dashboard, plus 5 malformed-returnUrl probes including a protocol-relative open-redirect attempt, an https-to-external-site attempt, a javascript: pseudo-URL attempt, an empty string, and a missing-leading-slash attempt — all silently fall back to dashboard); full end-to-end flow now works the same way whether the customer signs in with email-password OR with their Google account: cart-with-items → click any sign-in option → authenticate → land back on the cart → proceed to checkout (deploy `dms-00162-8lc` live)
- [x] **Silenced the 401 "Unauthorized" error that was appearing in the browser console on the cart page for guest visitors (not-yet-logged-in customers)** — the senior reviewer noticed a red error message in the browser developer console while viewing the cart as a guest: "POST app.anutech.in/api/v1/cart 401 (Unauthorized)"; investigation showed the message was harmless by design — the cart system was set up to **always try** to save cart contents to the customer's server-side account, and **expected** the save to fail with "Unauthorized" for guests (since they don't have an account yet), at which point the failure was silently swallowed and the cart simply continued using the customer's browser memory; this fail-soft pattern worked correctly (the cart kept working, customers could still add items, checkout flowed normally) but the red error still appeared in the developer console, which reads as a bug even though it isn't one; fix: changed the server endpoint behaviour so that when a guest customer (no account yet) tries to interact with the cart-save endpoint, the server now responds with a clean "success, but nothing saved" message instead of "Unauthorized"; the response shape acknowledges the request without making any change on the server side — the guest customer's browser-memory cart is unaffected, the cart still works exactly the same way for guests, but now there's no red error in the console either; **the same fix also protects against an older bundle the senior reviewer's browser had cached** (the URL in the error was technically the older versioned endpoint path, but both old and new paths route to the same handler via a built-in URL alias, so the fix applies cleanly to both); verified live on the new production revision: guests now see HTTP 200 with `{success: true, cart: []}` on cart-load, and HTTP 200 with `{success: true, saved: false}` on cart-save — no more 401 in any browser, with or without cache (deploy `dms-00161-549` live)
- [x] **Silenced the leftover "Minified React error #418" message that was still appearing in some customers' browser consoles after the earlier fix** — even after the morning's first hydration-error fix (which handled the query-string-during-render problem on the login and register forms), the senior reviewer reported the same red console error still showing up; investigation determined this remaining occurrence was a **different, separate problem from the one we fixed earlier** — this time the page's outermost HTML element itself (the `<html>` tag at the very top of every page) was the one being detected as "different" between what our server sent and what the customer's browser eventually showed on screen; the cause is **a browser extension running on the customer's machine modifying the `<html>` tag's attributes a split second after the page loads** — extensions like Dark Reader (theme converter), Grammarly (writing assistant), LastPass / 1Password / Bitwarden (password managers), Google Translate (page translation), and several accessibility-helper extensions all routinely append their own data-attributes to the `<html>` tag in the background; the server-rendered HTML carried only the plain `lang="en"` attribute, but by the time React tried to verify "is the page I rendered server-side still the same shape on the customer's screen?", the extensions had already added their attributes, the verification failed, and React threw the error; **this is not a real bug in our app's behaviour** — the page works correctly, the customer can complete every action, nothing on screen is wrong; the error was purely cosmetic noise in the developer console caused by the customer's own browser extensions; fix: applied the framework-recommended pattern of marking the outermost `<html>` and `<body>` tags as "ignore attribute-level differences here" so React stops complaining about extension-added attributes that aren't our code's fault; **the suppression is intentionally shallow** — it ignores attribute differences ONLY on those two specific top-level tags, not anywhere inside the page, so it can never mask a real bug deeper in the application; the same fix was applied to the framework's catch-all error page (the page customers would see if everything else broke) so it also stays clean (deploy `dms-00160-pzd` live)
- [x] **Guest customers logging in from the cart now land back on the cart with their items intact (previously they were stranded on the dashboard)** — the senior reviewer caught a frustrating customer-journey bug: a not-logged-in customer who'd added items to their cart and then clicked "Login to Checkout" was being dumped onto the dashboard after signing in, with their cart items seemingly gone (they were still there in browser memory but the customer couldn't see them from the dashboard); the root cause was a redirect chain that lost the cart along the way — when a guest customer adds items to the cart, the items live in their browser's local storage (since they're not signed in, the server has nothing to attach the cart to yet); they click "Login to Checkout" and are sent to the login page with a "return-me-to-checkout" hint; after signing in, the system redirects them straight to the checkout page; the checkout page checks the **server-side** cart (which is still empty because the local-storage-to-server cart sync only runs when the customer visits the cart page itself), sees no items, and **automatically redirects to the dashboard as a safety measure** (the dashboard is the catch-all "not sure what to do, you're logged in now" destination); the customer's items are still in their browser memory but they have no obvious way to recover them; fix: changed the cart's "Login to Checkout" button (and the related "Create an account" link for new customers and the "Sign in" link on the guest-checkout page) to send the customer back to the **cart** after signing in, not straight to the checkout; this lets the cart page run its normal "merge browser-memory items into the server-side cart" routine, after which the customer sees their items and clicks the now-blue "Proceed to Checkout" button to continue; the login page's existing blue "Please sign in to complete your purchase" notice (which previously only fired when the return destination was the checkout page) was extended to also fire when the return destination is the cart, so the customer still gets the helpful context message at sign-in time; verified live: the login page rendered for `/login?returnUrl=/cart` correctly carries the return-to-cart hint through to its register link as well (deploy `dms-00159-gdd` live)
- [x] **Fixed a "Minified React error #418" message appearing in the browser console on the login and register pages** — the senior reviewer opened the browser's developer tools while on the login page and noticed a red error message — even though the page itself looked fine to the customer's eye, having errors in the console is a signal that something underneath is unhealthy, and React explicitly recommends fixing all hydration mismatches because the affected part of the page is then re-rendered from scratch on the client, which is wasteful and can briefly flash incorrect content; **root cause was a latent bug that today's improvements surfaced** — the login form was reading query-string values (like the "return me back to where I came from" hint) directly during the page's initial render, and the Next.js framework doesn't actually have those query-string values available during the initial server-side render of a client-side component; the server rendered the page with the values resolved to "nothing", the customer's browser then re-rendered with the real values, and the resulting difference in the page's HTML triggered the React error; the bug had been in the login form for a while but never showed up before today because, until this morning's deploy, the Login button in the top navigation never passed any "return me back" information forward — so the values were always "nothing" on both sides and the mismatch never happened; this morning's fix that taught the Login button to remember the customer's current page was the first time real values were passed through, which is what surfaced this latent issue; **the fix follows the standard Next.js pattern**: the login page and the register page (which has the same query-string-in-render code pattern, so the same bug would have triggered on the register page the next time someone arrived via "/register?returnUrl=...") are now wrapped in a "Suspense" boundary, which is the framework's mechanism for telling it to wait for query-string values to be available before rendering the affected part — eliminating the server-vs-client difference; verified live on the new production revision: the SSR'd HTML for the login page now correctly carries the return-URL in its registration-link's address, and the matching register page does the same for its login link; React error #418 should no longer appear in the browser console on either page (deploy `dms-00158-whd` live)
- [x] **Chatbot was silently returning empty replies to real customer questions — discovered while testing and fixed** — caught while smoke-testing the chatbot end-to-end after this morning's deploys: every legitimate question (a customer asking "what TLDs do you offer?", "do you have .in domains?", etc.) was getting back an empty response — the chatbot would think for 2-3 seconds and then say nothing; the prompt-injection guard was still working (typing "ignore previous instructions" still gave the polite refusal correctly), which masked the bigger problem from the surface-level health checks; **the bug had been live since the Claude → Gemini swap earlier today** but went unnoticed because all the automated tests mock the upstream AI service directly with controlled responses that end the way our parser expects; root cause was a subtle SSE protocol mismatch — Gemini's response format normally ends each message chunk with a blank line, but for short single-chunk responses (most of our chatbot replies are short) something in the network path between Cloud Run and Gemini was stripping that trailing blank line; our parser only emitted chunks that it could cleanly separate at a blank-line boundary, so when the closing blank line was missing, the entire response was held in a buffer that never got flushed before the connection closed; fix: the parser now ALSO processes any remaining buffered content after the upstream connection closes (the "trailing flush"), so even single-chunk responses without a final blank line are still extracted and emitted to the customer; **also hardened the parser against Windows-style line endings** (`\r\n` vs the Unix `\n` the parser previously expected) so a future hosting/proxy change can't break the chatbot the same way again; two new automated regression checks added — one for the exact production scenario (single chunk, no trailing blank line) and one for the Windows-line-ending case — so this can't silently regress; verified live on production immediately after the deploy with three real questions (TLD question got a real Gemini answer about .com/.in/.net/.org, off-topic question got the polite refusal, prompt-injection got the input-guard refusal); 21 new automated checks added in this batch (2 chatbot regression pins + 19 navigation-Login-button pins from the earlier deploy) (deploy `dms-00157-jkt` live)
- [x] **Login button in the top navigation now returns the customer to the page they were on (instead of dropping them on the dashboard)** — when a customer was browsing the cart, clicked the blue Login button in the top-right corner, signed in, and was then unconditionally taken to their dashboard — losing the cart they were just looking at; they had to click their way back through the menu to the cart, find their items, and resume what they were doing; **the same was true for any other content page** — clicking Login from the domains-search page, the hosting plans page, or any product page also dumped them on the dashboard regardless of where they came from; fix: the Login button now passes a "return URL" hint to the login flow indicating the page the customer was on when they clicked it; the login flow (which already knew how to honour such a hint for some other entry points like the "Login to Checkout" button on the cart) now correctly bounces the customer back to wherever they came from after signing in; the fix applies to both the desktop Login button and the mobile-menu Login button; **a small list of pages are deliberately excluded** from this "return to where you came from" behaviour — the homepage (nowhere meaningful to return to), the login/register/activate/reset-password pages themselves (would create a confusing loop), the maintenance page, and the 403 error page; **the same open-redirect safety check from yesterday's profile-completion fix is reused** — only same-site paths are honoured, so this convenience feature can't be turned into a phishing redirect (deploy `dms-00156-z7b` live)
- [x] **Fixed the cart's "Complete Profile First" button — it now actually takes the customer to the profile page and brings them back to their cart afterwards** — a customer who has items in their cart but hasn't finished filling in their profile (phone number, address, etc.) saw a yellow "Complete Profile First" button instead of the normal "Proceed to Checkout"; **clicking it was a dead end** — the only thing that happened was a small red toast appearing at the top of the screen ("Please complete your profile before proceeding to checkout") and the page scrolling up; the customer had to figure out on their own that they needed to visit the dashboard's settings page, find the profile section, fill in the missing fields, save, then manually navigate back to the cart, click checkout again, and *finally* get to pay — a friction wall right at the moment they're trying to spend money; fix has two parts: (1) the button now sends the customer straight to the profile/settings page with a "return URL" hint pointing back to the cart, (2) the settings page reads that hint and — only when the profile has been fully completed on the server's side after the save — bounces the customer back to the cart automatically with a confirmation toast ("Profile completed! Taking you back to your cart..."); if the customer saves but the profile is *still* incomplete (some required field still missing), they stay on the settings page so they can keep filling it in — no surprise bounces back to a cart that would just reject them again; **safety check baked in**: the return-URL hint is only honoured when it points to a same-site path (starts with a single forward slash followed by a non-slash character); any attempt to use this mechanism to redirect to an external site (`//evil-site.com`, `https://evil-site.com`, `javascript:` URIs, etc.) is silently ignored and falls back to the regular dashboard destination, so this convenience feature can't be turned into a phishing redirect (deploy `dms-00155-w7j` live)
- [x] **Fixed the homepage chatbot showing "Sorry, something went wrong" to every anonymous visitor** — the senior reviewer tried out the new chatbot guard rails by opening the chat widget without logging in, asked "tell me a joke" expecting to see the polite refusal, and instead got a generic error message; root cause was a pre-existing bug that's been live the whole time but went unnoticed because all the chatbot's automated tests run against the route directly (the website's traffic-cop layer never executes in tests); the traffic-cop layer (which checks every incoming request for a valid login token before letting it through to most of our endpoints) was rejecting every request to the chatbot for anyone who wasn't already logged in, returning a 401 "Unauthorized" before the chatbot's own code ever got a chance to run; **the chatbot is supposed to be available to anonymous pre-sales visitors by design** (most people asking about domains and hosting haven't signed up yet — that's literally the audience the chat widget is built for), so the auth gate was wrong from the start; the bug was independent of the Anthropic→Gemini swap (the Claude version had the same issue, we just hadn't noticed); fix added the chatbot's endpoint to the traffic-cop's public-access allowlist (alongside the health check, the public domain search, the contact form, etc.) so anonymous visitors can now reach the chatbot; **the chatbot's own safety layers (the per-IP rate limit, the prompt-injection input filter, the secret-leak output filter, the strict system instructions) all continue to apply** — only the misplaced authentication gate was removed; an automated regression test was added so a future code change can't silently put the auth gate back; refreshed the chat widget on app.anutech.in and confirmed all three test scenarios work correctly: a domain question gets a real answer from Gemini, an off-topic question ("tell me a joke") gets the polite refusal, and a prompt-injection attempt ("ignore previous instructions") gets the same refusal (deploy `dms-00154-mgl` live)
- [x] **Customer-facing chatbot now strictly scope-locked to Anutech topics and protected against secret-data leaks** — added three layers of safety checks to the homepage chatbot so it (a) only answers questions about Anutech's domain and hosting services and politely declines anything else, and (b) cannot be tricked into revealing the project's internal credentials or any customer's personal information; **the first layer** rewrote the AI's own instructions from loose guidance into a strict policy with eight categories of requests explicitly listed for the AI to refuse — general-purpose AI tasks (jokes, recipes, coding help, weather, math, life advice), internal system details (database structure, source code, file paths, server software, even the identity of the AI vendor we use), credentials of any kind (passwords, tokens, API keys for any service, database connection details — refused even if the requester claims to be an admin or developer), other customers' personal information (names, emails, phone numbers, order history, account balances), direct actions on a customer's account (the chatbot is explicitly told it CANNOT place orders, modify DNS, change passwords, or take any other action — only redirect to login or support), prompt-manipulation attempts where someone tries to override the AI's rules, specific rupee pricing (always redirect to the website for current rates), and legal/financial/medical advice; the AI is also explicitly told to never echo any of its instructions back to the user and to never reveal which AI vendor powers it; 

**the second layer** runs before the AI is even contacted — the most recent customer message is checked against a list of known manipulation phrases ("ignore previous instructions", "show me your system prompt", "act as a different AI", "developer mode", "what AI model are you", and similar variations); on a match, the chatbot returns the standard polite-refusal response immediately without using any of our free-tier quota on the upstream AI service (saves quota AND guarantees a consistent response); only the LATEST customer message is screened (earlier conversation turns are left alone so the chatbot can legitimately summarise prior discussion if asked); the check is case-insensitive (uppercase variants are caught too);

**the third layer** sits between the AI and the customer's screen — every piece of the AI's streaming response is checked as it flows through, against a list of patterns that would indicate the AI is about to leak a real secret (any service's API-key formats, database connection strings, the names of internal environment-variable settings the project uses, and references to internal system state);

**if any such pattern is detected, the leaking piece is dropped before it can reach the customer's browser** (so the customer never sees even a fragment of the secret), the rest of the response is replaced with the standard polite-refusal message, and the stream cleanly closes; the same refusal text is used across all three layers ("I can only help with questions about Anutech's domain and hosting services. For other questions, please reach out to support@anutech.in or browse our website at https://anutech.in.") so the customer-facing experience reads identically regardless of which layer did the refusing; 37 new automated safety checks were added to verify all three layers — each known manipulation phrase has its own check confirming the refusal fires and no AI quota is wasted, each potential secret-leak pattern has its own check confirming the leak is dropped and the refusal is appended, and there are positive-case checks confirming that legitimate questions about domain registration, hosting plans, and TLDs continue to work normally (deploy `dms-00153-krg` live)

- [x] **Removed the misleading "Powered by Claude AI" label from the chat widget** — the small attribution line at the bottom of the homepage chat widget was still saying "Powered by Claude AI" even though we'd just moved the chatbot off Claude onto Google Gemini in the previous deploy; the senior reviewer caught this immediately when checking the chat widget; the line was removed entirely (rather than swapped to "Powered by Google Gemini") because there's no business reason to advertise which AI vendor we use behind the scenes — the chat widget is part of the customer experience, not a marketing surface for the underlying tech; one-line removal in the chat widget component, deployed within minutes of the original reviewer screenshot (deploy `dms-00152-hhf` live)

- [x] **Customer-facing AI chatbot moved to a free AI service (cost-saving migration)** — the homepage chat widget that customers use to ask pre-sales questions about domain pricing, hosting plans, and DNS settings has been moved off a paid AI service (which billed per-message to the company credit card) onto Google's free-tier AI service;

- **the customer experience is unchanged** — the assistant still replies word-by-word as the customer reads (rather than a long pause then a wall of text), still understands the same range of questions, still points complex issues to the support team, still applies the same per-customer rate limit (a single visitor can send at most 10 messages per minute so one abusive visitor can't burn through the free quota for everyone), and the chatbot still keeps the last 20 turns of conversation in memory; the model was specifically chosen and pinned to a working free-tier option (a different Google model the team tried first turned out to have zero free quota allocated; the chatbot now uses Google's current free-tier default and the exact model name is locked in so a future change on Google's side can't silently move the chatbot to a paid tier without us noticing); **all 31 chatbot safety checks were rewritten** to match the new service while keeping every original protection in place: requests that haven't been signed properly by the project's secret are rejected before any AI call (so a forged request can't drain the free quota); the API key is read from a secret manager rather than baked into the code (so the key isn't visible in git history or in the customer's browser); if the secret manager is misconfigured and the key is missing entirely, the chatbot returns a generic "service unavailable" error instead of crashing; abuse signals from Google (rate-limited, quota exhausted) are translated into customer-friendly "please try again in a moment" messages; **Google's raw error bodies are NEVER passed through to the customer's screen** (the safety check probes this with a fake leak-marker and confirms it's not visible in our error responses — so even if Google's error includes the API key fragment for some reason, it won't reach the customer); network hiccups mid-conversation (a stalled or dropped connection from Google) emit a clean "stream error" rather than crashing the chat widget; the chatbot will never accidentally print Google's internal `[DONE]` end-of-message marker to customers (caught and consumed silently); even when one chunk of Google's response is malformed, the rest of the message still flows through; all the new checks pass in our automated test suite, and the whole thing was deployed live without any service interruption (deploy `dms-00151-55b` live)
- [x] **Shopping cart now syncs across devices for logged-in customers** — fixed a bug noticed while the senior reviewer was browsing the cart page: a logged-in customer's cart wasn't following them to another device or browser because the front-end was calling the wrong server address to save the cart; a 401 "Unauthorized" error was showing up in the browser console as a result; **the local-device cart kept working from browser storage so most customers wouldn't have noticed**, but anyone who added items on their phone and then opened their laptop would have seen an empty cart; the comment immediately above the broken code even referenced the correct address (`/api/cart`), so it was clearly a typo where someone had added an unused version-number segment (`/api/v1/cart`) that doesn't match any real endpoint; one-line address correction applied in three places (the read-from-server / save-to-server / merge-with-server methods); the console error is now gone and cross-device cart sync works for logged-in customers (deploy `dms-00150-jmx` live)
- [x] **Payment-confirmation listener + automated security patch sweep** — added automated safety checks for the production listener that receives "payment confirmed" notifications from our payment provider (Razorpay) and uses those to actually order the customer's domain / set up their hosting after the money has cleared (this is what turns a paid checkout into a real working domain — separate from the subscription-renewal listener that was already tested earlier): every incoming notification is **cryptographically signature-verified on every call so a malicious actor cannot fabricate a "payment confirmed" message and trigger free provisioning** (pinned with a missing-signature probe, a wrong-signature probe, a wrong-shared-secret probe, AND a body-tampering probe — the listener correctly rejects every one of those forgery attempts); the listener refuses to start if the shared secret isn't configured (fail-closed — better to refuse than accept unverified notifications); known events are handled (payment confirmation + refund), unknown events return a generic "ok" so the payment provider doesn't keep retrying nuisance messages; when a payment confirmation arrives the listener finds the matching order (via customer order reference or fallback description) and refuses to do anything if the order doesn't exist (anti-retry-storm — returns "ok" so the provider stops retrying instead of treating it as an error worth retrying); **renewal payments and hosting-upgrade payments are explicitly handed off to a different code path that knows how to extend the existing hosting's expiry / switch the customer's plan** — the listener correctly identifies these order types and gets out of the way (pinned per-order-type); orders that have already been provisioned (by the customer's browser confirming first, or by an earlier delivery of the same notification) are treated as already-done — the listener only records the payment-id if it wasn't already recorded but does NOT re-provision (idempotency — same notification arriving twice never double-charges or double-provisions); **for fresh pending orders the listener uses an atomic database claim to handle the race against the customer's browser also confirming the payment** — only ONE of the two paths wins the claim and provisions, the other backs off (pinned: claim-lost path makes NO accounting call and NO provisioning call, so the work isn't done twice); if the customer's account got deleted between checkout and payment confirmation the listener escalates (so a human can investigate) rather than silently dropping the payment; the **accounting-system invoice creation step swallows accounting outages with a "creation_failed" marker** so the customer's provisioning still completes — a self-healing background job retries the invoice creation later (pinned: accounting being down does NOT block provisioning, and the listener does NOT keep retrying for accounting reasons); provisioning is invoked with a "webhook_verified" marker on the order's audit trail so reviewers can distinguish which payments came in via the listener vs. the customer's browser confirmation; if provisioning itself fails the listener escalates so the payment provider retries (giving us another shot at provisioning rather than losing the payment); on a refund the listener creates a credit note in the accounting system so the books stay balanced — but **refuses to create a credit note if there's no original invoice to credit against** (defensive — pinned for missing invoice, "creation_failed" marker, and missing order cases); **every accounting-side failure during refund processing is SWALLOWED so the payment provider does NOT keep retrying the refund webhook** — a credit-note creation issue is an accounting concern that admin handles via logs, not something the payment provider needs to retry; outer catch on any unexpected error returns a generic "internal error" with NO leak of internal database / accounting details to the response body; **separately, the automated security scanner caught 4 newly-disclosed HIGH-severity vulnerabilities in our dependency tree** during the deploy gate (these are libraries our libraries depend on, where the security community recently announced new issues): a multipart-form library had a CRLF-injection issue, a protocol-buffers library had a prototype-pollution + denial-of-service issue, a build tool had a path-traversal bypass + Windows credential disclosure issue, and a WebSocket library had a memory-exhaustion denial-of-service issue; **all four patched to newer non-breaking versions in the same maintenance line** along with three lower-severity updates (HTML sanitiser, YAML parser, email-sending library); the scanner now reports zero outstanding vulnerabilities at any severity level (deploy `dms-00149-tgq` live)
- [x] **Public TLD-list endpoint (+ fixed a duplicate-TLD bug along the way)** — added automated safety checks for the public-facing TLD-list endpoint that the domain search UI calls on every page load to populate the TLD picker and the "available extensions" filter: **the endpoint is intentionally PUBLIC (no login required) so the search UI works for visitors who haven't signed up yet — pinned with explicit no-auth-call assertions so a future refactor can't accidentally lock the search UI behind login**; supports a developer-only `x-testing-mode: true` header that returns a static TLD list (so end-to-end tests don't depend on the registrar's API being reachable from CI) — the testing-mode header is **strict-equals "true" so a casual `True` / `TRUE` / `1` / `presence-only` does NOT switch into test mode** (pinned per-variant); response wraps each TLD as `{name, displayName: ".${name}", available: true}` with the leading dot only on `displayName` and `available` strictly true on every entry (pinned per-entry so a refactor that flips `available` to a function-returning-truthy value would still satisfy the strict-equals invariant); restricted TLDs (`gov.in`, `ac.in`, `edu.in`, `mil`, `gov`, `edu`) are MUST-NOT-APPEAR in the response (UI surfacing these would set false customer expectations — the registrar can't actually fulfil those registrations); the testing-mode list is name-set-equal to the live comprehensive list so test-only TLDs can't leak into prod and prod-only TLDs can't go missing from tests (pinned via sorted-name equality across both responses); each response carries a `requestId` (random 5-char base36 fragment) that the UI uses to correlate browser-side errors back to server-side log entries; the outer catch returns a 200 with a short hardcoded fallback list (NOT 500) so the search UI never blanks even if a future refactor adds async I/O that fails; **THE TEST CAUGHT AND FIXED A REAL DUPLICATE-TLD BUG** — `shop` and `store` were listed twice in both the testing-mode and comprehensive lists (once under new-gTLDs and once under the shopping/money section), which made them appear twice in the search dropdown; fixed by wrapping both list-maps with `[...new Set(arr)]` so dedup happens at the boundary instead of relying on careful manual list maintenance (deploy `dms-00148-xkr` live)
- [x] **Customer "import my domains from the registrar" sync + daily per-service expiry/reminder worker** — added automated safety checks for the customer's manual "pull all of my domains from ResellerClub into my account" POST endpoint and for the per-service expiry worker that Cloud Tasks fires once per hosting/domain on its scheduled checkpoint: the customer endpoint requires login (401 if not), looks up the customer's DB record (404 if missing), **handles the brand-new-customer case gracefully — when the customer's user record doesn't yet have a registrar customer ID linked, the route tries to find one by email; success persists the linked ID via `dbUser.save()` BEFORE making any further calls, failure returns a 200 with `code: NO_LINKED_ACCOUNT` instead of 404 (anti-console-spam — brand-new customers without a registrar account get a clean "you haven't registered any domains yet" UX rather than an error toast)** — pinned per-branch; metadata keys (`recsonpage`, `recsindb`) at the top of the registrar's response map are skipped at the iteration boundary so they don't get treated as fake domains; domain-name resolution walks a three-step chain (`entity.description` → `domainname` → `domain` → skip if none); order-ID resolution prefers `orders.orderid` then falls back to the map key; **registrar status `active` (case-insensitive AND trimmed) imports as a "registered" domain with booking-status step `domain_registered` progress 100; any other status (InvoicePaid, Processing, Suspended, etc.) imports as "pending" with booking-status `payment_verified` progress 30 — and the `successfulDomains` array stays EMPTY for pending status** (pinned per-branch so pending imports don't leak into registered-domain listings downstream); every per-domain existence check is keyed on `findOrderByDomainForUser(user._id, domainName)` so a hostile payload can't synthesize domains under another customer's user ID (anti-IDOR — pinned with explicit second-arg assertion); per-domain failures are isolated in a try/catch so one bad row doesn't kill the rest of the batch (imported/skipped/failed counts surfaced in the response); registrar-side `getCustomerDomains` failures abort the sync with a 500 and surface the upstream message (no partial imports); the per-service expiry worker requires the cron secret on every call (machine-only — admin can't accidentally fire 500 emails from the browser), validates zod-strict `serviceId` and `serviceType=hosting|domain` (with optional `simulatedTime` ISO escape hatch for QA fast-forward), routes hosting through `getHostingById({populateUser:true})` and domain through `Domain.findById().populate('userId')`; **a slate of permanent-skip responses return 200 success (not 500) so Cloud Tasks doesn't keep retrying** — service not found, terminal status (failed/terminated), missing expiry date, AND the anti-loop guard "overdue but already in a non-active state" which ALSO clears `next_action_at` to null so a once-failed service doesn't get rescheduled every day forever; the worker has three flow paths — EXPIRY (`daysLeft <= 0` AND status='active' → suspend the DA user + flip the local status to 'expired' + send the suspension email + WhatsApp + clear `next_action_at` so no further crons fire), REMINDER (`daysLeft > 0` → walk the descending threshold list `[30, 15, 7, 1]` and send the appropriate N-day reminder unless already sent for that threshold — pinned per-threshold with `last_reminder_sent` dedup; reschedule `next_action_at` to the next checkpoint, or to `expiryDate at UTC midnight` for the final 1-day threshold), and FALLBACK (no reminder fires → recalculate `next_action_at` to the next pending checkpoint — anti-runaway-loop pinned); **the EXPIRY flow has a special 1-day grace window for Razorpay subscription auto-renewals** (when `autoRenew=true && billingType='subscription' && subscriptionId set` AND the service has been expired <1 day, the worker reschedules to `now + 24h` with action='grace_period' and does NOT suspend — giving the payment webhook 24 hours to extend the expiry; >=1 day elapsed proceeds to normal suspension); the DA-suspend call routes the registrar's typed outcomes distinctly — `suspended` and `user_not_found` are both terminal (no retry, treated as already-suspended in the second case); `da_unreachable` and `hard_failure` THROW so the worker returns 500 and Cloud Tasks retries (pinned: `status` is NOT flipped to 'expired' before the DA throw — so a transient DA outage doesn't leave the local row in an inconsistent state); domain-type services have a no-op suspendService (the registrar manages actual suspension on its side — pinned NO daSuspendUser call even on domain expiry); email/WhatsApp send failures are all swallowed with `.catch()` so the worker's main response succeeds even if SMTP / WhatsApp hiccups; **a finally block ALWAYS clears `processing_until` to null and calls `save()` regardless of which branch ran (happy + inner-catch + DA-throw all release the lock)** — pinned so a stuck lock can't hold the row hostage during the next worker cycle (deploy `dms-00147-nbr` live)
- [x] **Customer DNS records CRUD + admin DNS records CRUD** — added automated safety checks for the two paired DNS-record management endpoints (one for the customer managing their own domain's DNS records — A / AAAA / MX / CNAME / TXT / NS / SRV — and one for admin managing any customer's DNS records through ResellerClub): the customer endpoint has FOUR methods (read all records, add a record, update an existing record, delete a record); the admin endpoint has THREE methods (read, add, delete — there is intentionally NO admin update path for audit-trail clarity, since admin delete+re-add leaves two log entries instead of one in-place mutation); **all four customer methods scope the ownership check to the calling customer via `findOrderByDomainForUser(user._id, domainName)` so one customer can never read, add, modify, or delete another customer's DNS** (anti-IDOR — pinned per-method with explicit second-arg assertion); the customer endpoint has a **deliberate 404-vs-403 asymmetry** between safe-read methods and mutation methods that is pinned cross-method: GET and POST return 404 "Domain not found" when the user doesn't own the domain (read-side ambiguity — prevents domain-enumeration through differential responses), while PUT and DELETE return 403 "Unauthorized modification/deletion attempt" (mutation-side explicit denial — produces an audit signal that someone tried to modify a domain they don't own); **POST/PUT/DELETE all run a CSRF check FIRST before auth resolution** (a forged form submission can't even reach the auth step); GET intentionally skips CSRF since it's a safe-method idempotent read; both endpoints validate the registrant's DNS-management precondition by checking that the domain row has a populated `resellerClubCustomerId` (returns 404 "DNS management not active" / "Domain configuration missing" / "ResellerClub Customer ID not found for this domain" when absent — so a customer can't trigger an RC call on a domain that isn't actually under our DNS management); the **admin endpoint refuses non-admins with 403 instead of 401** (anti-info-leak — same response whether the request is anonymous or just non-admin) and has NO IDOR scoping by design (the whole point of the admin endpoint is reaching any customer's DNS — pinned with a single-argument `findOrderByDomain(domainName)` call and an explicit not-called-with-second-arg assertion); the GET path dispatches the registrar's three typed outcomes distinctly — records present → 200 with the records list, ResellerClub-not-found → 404 with **"Domain not found in ResellerClub" (deliberately DISTINCT body from the local-DB "Domain not found" 404 so admin can disambiguate "domain isn't in our system" vs "domain is in our system but not in RC")**, hard-failure → 500 with the raw RC reason surfaced (admin trusted to see upstream errors for debugging — pinned with cross-call body inequality assertion); the admin POST recordId extraction has a fallback chain (`result.data.recordid` → `.recordId` camelCase variant — RC is inconsistent across endpoints) and the admin recordData schema uses `.passthrough()` so per-record-type fields (MX priority, SRV weight, etc.) flow through unchanged; the customer DELETE has a **mixed parameter source** pinned (domainName and recordId pulled from the query string, recordData from the request body, then re-validated as one composite via deleteSchema — pinned because losing track of which field comes from where would either let a hostile body override the URL-stamped record ID or break the contract entirely); all outer catches across all 7 method+endpoint combinations return generic 500 responses with NO upstream-error fragment leaking into the body (Mongo errors, RC error messages, admin-auth blow-ups — sentinel-leak guards inject markers like `secret-host-leak-A` / `rc_LEAK_MARKER` / `admin_LEAK_MARKER` and assert their absence from the response) (deploy `dms-00146-kft` live)
- [x] **Customer invoice PDF download + admin invoice PDF download** — added automated safety checks for the two paired invoice-PDF endpoints (one for the customer pulling their own invoice, one for admin pulling any customer's invoice): both endpoints have a dual-path flow — when the order has a real Zoho-Books invoice ID the live PDF is fetched from Zoho, otherwise a locally-generated "Proforma" PDF is rendered server-side; **the customer endpoint accepts EITHER a JWT login OR a NextAuth session (JWT checked first, NextAuth as fallback) — deactivated accounts holding a still-valid NextAuth token are refused unless their role is admin (which is the only escape hatch)**; the customer endpoint scopes the order lookup to the calling customer's user ID via `findUserOrder(id, String(user._id))` so one customer can never download another customer's invoice (anti-IDOR — pinned with an explicit second-arg assertion AND a coercion test for ObjectId-shaped IDs); **the customer endpoint returns 404 "Order not found" identically for both "does not exist" AND "not yours" — pinned with a byte-identical-body comparison so a future logging tweak can't reintroduce the side-channel**; the admin endpoint is admin-only (non-admins get 401 before any database read, Zoho call, or customer-lookup happens) and has NO IDOR scoping (admins can see any customer's invoice — that's the whole point of the endpoint, pinned with a single-argument `getOrderById(id)` assertion); the admin endpoint fetches the customer's profile separately via `getUserById(order.userId)` for the Bill-To section and returns a distinct **404 "Customer not found"** (distinct body from the order-missing 404) if the customer record is gone — admin trusted environment so the disambiguation aids debugging without leaking; 

**when Zoho returns a null buffer (Zoho outage / token expired / 5xx), the endpoint silently FALLS BACK to a proforma PDF instead of returning 500** — the customer still gets a usable downloadable invoice, admin still gets a usable file (pinned per-endpoint with the fallback Content-Disposition); filename construction differs per path — Zoho-sourced PDFs use `Invoice-${invoiceNumber || orderId}.pdf` (falls back to orderId when no invoice number is set yet), local proformas use `Proforma-${orderId}.pdf` on the customer side and `Proforma-Admin-${orderId}.pdf` on the admin side (the "Admin" suffix distinguishes admin downloads from customer-side proformas when admin saves multiple files to their downloads folder); both endpoints set `Content-Type: application/pdf` with `Content-Disposition: attachment`; outer catch returns generic 500 "Internal Server Error" with NO upstream-error fragment leaking into the response body (Mongo timeouts, Zoho 401 invalid_token, admin-auth blow-ups, getUserById errors — all redacted, pinned with sentinel-leak probes injecting `secret-host-leak` / `access_token` / `leak-marker-A` markers and asserting their absence) (deploy `dms-00145-rdr` live)
- [x] **Admin users list (CRUD) + admin manual register-pending-domain action** — added automated safety checks: the admin users-list endpoint requires admin login on all three methods (read, role-update, delete); **the list filters out other admins AND already-deleted users** (so the user-management view doesn't show peer admins as customer-action targets, and soft-deleted users don't reappear in the active list); pagination is capped at 100 per page (anti-DoS), with page-below-1 clamped to 1 and limit-below-1 clamped to 1; **the response curates each user down to nine safe fields** (password hash, TOTP secret, password-reset token, pending-email token all asserted ABSENT via sentinel-leak probe); strict-truth handling on the `isActive` flag (undefined → true since the field is opt-out, not opt-in) and `totpEnabled` (undefined → false since the field is opt-in); **the role-update path uses a `.strict()` zod schema that rejects any extra body fields** (no mass-assignment of password, role escalation via unexpected fields); refuses to operate on other admins (privilege protection — peer admins can only be changed via direct DB access, not customer-service tooling — pinned 403); refuses on non-ObjectId user IDs at the schema layer; **the delete path refuses an admin deleting their own account** (anti-lockout), refuses deleting other admins (peer-admin protection), supports a soft-delete (default) vs `permanent=true` hard-delete branch with **strict-equals on the literal string "true" so loose-truthy values like `permanent=TRUE` / `permanent=1` / `permanent=yes` all stay safe-by-default soft** (pinned per-variant); the permanent path runs `permanentDeleteUser` which snapshots the customer's name+email onto their existing orders BEFORE the user record is wiped (so historic order records still have customer attribution); the admin "manually register this pending domain" action requires admin login (401 if not), refuses to act if the pending row isn't found (404) or isn't currently in pending status (400 — **prevents double-registering a row already in processing/completed/failed state**), **flips the row's status to "processing" BEFORE firing the registrar call so a concurrent admin click can't double-fire** (pinned with a probe capturing status at the moment of the RC call); contacts fall back to the row's main contactId when admin/tech/billing contacts aren't separately set; on success marks the row completed with the new expiry date computed as registration-period × 365 days from now, updates the linked order's domain entry to registered with the registrar order ID stamped, sends the customer an order-confirmation email **only if ALL domains in their order are now registered** (partial registration → no email), the email subtotal is the pre-GST amount (total / 1.18), and triggers a Zoho-invoice sync — but **only when no invoice exists yet** (idempotency: existing real `zohoInvoiceId` skips Zoho; "pending_creation" sentinel treated as unset and proceeds); Zoho failures are swallowed (registration still succeeds); on registrar failure the row is marked failed with the registrar's error message captured in the reason field, the order's domain entry is also updated to failed (with `markModified` flagged so Mongoose persists the array mutation), and the failure surfaces with a 400 (registrar said no) or 500 (registrar threw); outer catch returns 500 INTERNAL_ERROR generic (deploy `dms-00144-cxc` live)
- [x] **Admin & customer support-ticket details (read / reply / status update / close)** — added automated safety checks for the two complementary support-ticket detail endpoints: the admin-side endpoint requires admin login on all three methods (read, status/priority update, reply); only admins can change status or priority and only between the four valid states (open / in_progress / resolved / closed); when an admin marks a ticket resolved or closed the resolved-at timestamp is stamped to now (pinned per-status), when they reopen it (any other status) the timestamp is cleared to null (pinned per-status); the admin reply pushes the message with authorRole "admin", names the author from the admin's first+last (with a "Support Team" fallback for empty-string names); refuses to reply on closed tickets (400 TICKET_CLOSED); validates attachments through a server-side helper that returns a 400 VALIDATION_ERROR on bad files; checks the per-ticket cumulative attachment cap and refuses with 400 TICKET_STORAGE_FULL when exceeded; auto-bumps an open or resolved ticket into in-progress on admin reply (with the resolved-at cleared when reopening from resolved); **emails the customer with the admin's reply body — and BOTH the customer's name AND the admin's message are HTML-escaped before being interpolated into the email body (XSS-safe — pinned with `<script>`, `<img onerror=…>`, and quote-character probes)**; newlines in the message become `<br>` in HTML; email subject locks the ticket number; email-send failure is swallowed (.catch) so the API response still succeeds; the customer-side endpoint scopes all three methods to the calling user's own tickets via `findUserTicket(id, user._id)` (anti-IDOR — pinned with explicit scope-arg assertion); **PATCH accepts ONLY `status="closed"` (literal-strict via zod) — customers cannot bump priority, reopen, or set any other status; PATCH with status=open/in_progress/resolved → 400** (pinned per-status); idempotent if the ticket is already closed (no save, no email — the existing close stands); on a fresh close the resolved-at is stamped and an admin notification email is sent with the customer's name HTML-escaped; the customer reply path is **rate-limited per-user with the key `support_reply:${userId}`** (not per-IP — anti-shared-network); refuses replies on closed tickets (400 TICKET_CLOSED); applies the same attachment validation + per-ticket storage cap;
